=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There are an infinite amount of bags on a number line, one bag for each coordinate. Some of these bags contain coins.
You are given a 2D array coins, where coins[i] = [l_i, r_i, c_i] denotes that every bag from l_i to r_i contains c_i coins.
The segments that coins contain are non-overlapping.
You are also given an integer k.
Return the maximum amount of coins you can obtain by collecting k consecutive bags.
 
Example 1:

Input: coins = [[8,10,1],[1,3,2],[5,6,4]], k = 4
Output: 10
Explanation:
Selecting bags at positions [3, 4, 5, 6] gives the maximum number of coins: 2 + 0 + 4 + 4 = 10.

Example 2:

Input: coins = [[1,10,3]], k = 2
Output: 6
Explanation:
Selecting bags at positions [1, 2] gives the maximum number of coins: 3 + 3 = 6.

 
Constraints:

1 <= coins.length <= 10^5
1 <= k <= 10^9
coins[i] == [l_i, r_i, c_i]
1 <= l_i <= r_i <= 10^9
1 <= c_i <= 1000
The given segments are non-overlapping.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maximumCoins(self, coins: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Approach 1: Sweep all possible starts using events and segment tree? F(s) = sum of interval weights over sliding window. We can compute F(s) at all breakpoints by difference array over compressed coordinates? Coordinates up to 1e9 but intervals n=1e5. We can compress boundaries l_i, r_i+1, l_i-k, r_i+1-k? Actually F(s) is linear between breakpoints, so max at breakpoints. We can evaluate F at O(n) candidate starts. Need ensure candidate set includes all maxima. We can include all breakpoints where slope changes: l_i, r_i+1, l_i-k, r_i+1-k? Let's verify discrete. For F(s) over integer s, difference Δ(s)=F(s+1)-F(s) = A(s+k)-A(s). Let's implement F(s) carefully.

Data structures:
coins list sorted by l. Let n = len(coins)
l_list = [l_i], r_list = [r_i], c_list = [c_i], length = r-l+1, total_i = length*c_i.
pref = [0]*(n+1)
for i in range(n): pref[i+1] = pref[i] + (r_i-l_i+1)*c_i

Function calc(s):
    e = s + k - 1
    # find first interval with r >= s
    a = bisect_left(r_list, s)
    if a == n:
        return 0
    # find last interval with l <= e
    b = bisect_right(l_list, e) - 1
    if b < a:
        return 0
    if a == b:
        left = max(l_list[a], s)
        right = min(r_list[a], e)
        if left > right: return 0 (shouldn't if b>=a? Let's verify if a==b but no intersection possible? a first r>=s, b last l<=e. If a==b, then interval a has r>=s and l<=e. Since l<=r, intersection nonempty? Need also l<=e and r>=s. Yes because l<=e and r>=s, so max(l,s) <= min(r,e)? Since l<=r and s<=r, e>=l; but need s<=e? Window length k>=1 so s<=e. max(l,s) <= min(r,e) because l<=e and s<=r. Yes.)
        return c_list[a] * (right - left + 1)
    # full intervals from a+1 to b-1 inclusive
    total = pref[b] - pref[a+1]  # pref index: pref[i] sum intervals [0,i-1]; sum [a+1, b-1] = pref[b] - pref[a+1]
    # partial a: suffix from max(l_a,s) to r_a
    left_a = max(l_list[a], s)
    total += c_list[a] * (r_list[a] - left_a + 1)
    # partial b: prefix from l_b to min(r_b,e)
    right_b = min(r_list[b], e)
    total += c_list[b] * (right_b - l_list[b] + 1)
    return total

Need ensure pref[b] - pref[a+1] doesn't include a or b. Example a=0,b=2: full interval 1: pref[2]-pref[1] = total interval1. Good. If b=a+1: pref[a+1]-pref[a+1]=0. Good.

Now, is there a possibility that F(s) maximum occurs at s not in C because F(s) is not concave and has multiple local maxima, but every local max in C. We argued.

Let's formalize proof for myself:
A(p) is integer step function. Δ(s)=F(s+1)-F(s). If s is not in C, then none of the jump points of A are at s or s+k. Therefore A(s) and A(s+k) are locally constant as functions of s? More precisely, for t in a small interval around s not containing any jump point, Δ(t) is constant. Thus Δ(s-1)=Δ(s). If F(s) is a strict local maximum, need Δ(s-1)>0 and Δ(s)<0, impossible. If F(s) is a non-strict maximum at plateau endpoint, then Δ(s-1)!=Δ(s) at endpoint, so s in C. If F(s) is global max and plateau extends beyond candidate? If plateau has no endpoints? impossible. If plateau endpoints not in C? At endpoint, Δ changes, so endpoint in C. If plateau includes all s? no. Thus max in C. Good.

Need maybe include s = l_i - k + 1? Let's re-evaluate "jump points of A are at s or s+k" for Δ(s-1) vs Δ(s). Δ(s-1) = A(s-1+k) - A(s-1) = A(s+k-1) - A(s-1). Δ(s) = A(s+k) - A(s). Difference Δ(s)-Δ(s-1) = [A(s+k)-A(s+k-1)] + [A(s-1)-A(s)]? Actually = A(s+k)-A(s) - A(s+k-1)+A(s-1) = (A(s+k)-A(s+k-1)) + (A(s-1)-A(s)). The terms change when s+k is jump point (s = jump - k) or s is jump point (s = jump). So yes. The difference can be nonzero at s = jump or jump-k. If s not in C, Δ(s)=Δ(s-1). Good.

Let's prove 2 candidates more rigorously:
Take any optimal window [s, s+k-1]. If s is not a left endpoint l_i:
- If s is in a gap (no interval covers s), then A(s)=0. Moving window right by 1 changes F by A(s+k) - A(s) = A(s+k) >=0. So F(s+1) >= F(s). Final code:
```python
from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left, bisect_right

class Solution:
    def maximumCoins(self, coins: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        coins.sort(key=lambda x: x[0])
        n = len(coins)
        left = [c[0] for c in coins]
        right = [c[1] for c in coins]
        val = [c[2] for c in coins]
        pref = [0]*(n+1)
        for i,(l,r,c) in enumerate(coins):
            pref[i+1] = pref[i] + (r-l+1)*c
        
        def calc(s: int) -> int:
            e = s + k - 1
            a = bisect_left(right, s)
            if a == n:
                return 0
            b = bisect_right(left, e) - 1
            if b < a:
                return 0
            if a == b:
                l = left[a]; r = right[a]
                lo = l if l > s else s
                hi = r if r < e else e
                return val[a] * (hi - lo + 1)
            total = pref[b] - pref[a+1]
            # partial a
            l = left[a]; r = right[a]
            lo = l if l > s else s
            total += val[a] * (r - lo + 1)
            # partial b
            l = left[b]; r = right[b]
            hi = r if r < e else e
            total += val[b] * (hi - l + 1)
            return total
        
        ans = 0
        seen = set()
        for l,r,c in coins:
            for s in (l, r - k + 1):
                if s not in seen:
                    seen.add(s)
                    ans = max(ans, calc(s))
        return ans
```

Let W(s) = [s, s+k-1]. Suppose W(s) is optimal. If s is not a left endpoint of any interval:
- If W(s) starts in a gap, then position s has 0 coins. Moving to s+1 removes 0 coins and adds coins at s+k (maybe 0 or positive). So W(s+1) has at least as many coins. Repeat until s is a left endpoint or window passes all intervals. If it passes all intervals, coins 0, not optimal. Thus there is an optimal window starting at a left endpoint.
- Otherwise s lies strictly inside an interval A. Let e=s+k-1. If e is not a right endpoint of any interval:
   * If position e has fewer coins than position s-1? Actually moving left by 1: new window [s-1, e-1]. Change = coins at s-1 - coins at e. Since s is strictly inside A, coins at s-1 = c_A. If coins at e < c_A, moving left increases coins, contradiction. Thus coins at e >= c_A.
   * Since e is not a right endpoint, if coins at e >= c_A, then position e+1 has at least coins at e (if e inside an interval, same c; if e in gap, coins at e=0 < c_A impossible). Moving right by 1: change = coins at e+1 - coins at s. coins at s = c_A, coins at e+1 >= c_A, so moving right does not decrease. Repeat until either s reaches a left endpoint or e reaches a right endpoint. If s reaches left endpoint, candidate l_i. If e reaches right endpoint, s = r_i-k+1 candidate. If both happen, candidate either.
This proof assumes if e is not right endpoint and coins at e >= c_A, then coins at e+1 >= coins at e. Is that always true? If e is in a gap, coins at e=0, cannot be >= c_A>0. So e must be inside an interval B. If e < r_B, then coins at e+1 = c_B = coins at e >= c_A. If e = r_B, then e is right endpoint, contradiction. So yes. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possibility that partial a should be `val[a] * (min(r_a, e) - max(l_a, s) + 1)` instead of our simplified `val[a] * (r_a - max(l_a,s) + 1)`. Since a first r>=s, e>=? e >= s, but e may be less than r_a? If a==b and e inside a, then partial a should use min(r_a,e). In a==b we use hi=min(r,e). In a<b, is it possible e < r_a? If a<b, then b is after a, and l_b > r_a (non-overlap). Since b last l<=e, we have l_b <= e. Thus e >= l_b > r_a. So e > r_a. Therefore min(r_a,e)=r_a. So simplified okay. But to be safe and general, we could use `min(r_a, e)` in partial a too. It won't hurt. Let's use general formula for clarity:
partial_a = val[a] * (min(r_a, e) - max(l_a, s) + 1)
partial_b = val[b] * (min(r_b, e) - max(l_b, s) + 1)
Then if a==b, this would double count if we add both; handle a==b separately. For a<b, partial_a min(r_a,e) = r_a because e >= l_b > r_a, but general formula okay. Use general to avoid relying. Similarly partial_b max(l_b,s) = l_b because s <= r_a < l_b? If a<b, s <= r_a < l_b, so max=l_b. But general okay.
Let's implement calc with general formula:
```
if a == b:
    lo = max(left[a], s)
    hi = min(right[a], e)
    return val[a] * (hi - lo + 1)
total = pref[b] - pref[a+1]
lo_a = max(left[a], s)
hi_a = min(right[a], e)
total += val[a] * (hi_a - lo_a + 1)
lo_b = max(left[b], s)
hi_b = min(right[b], e)
total += val[b] * (hi_b - lo_b + 1)
return total
```
This is more robust and clear. Since a<b, hi_a = r_a, lo_b=l_b, but no matter. If somehow hi_a < lo_a? Not possible. But if a<b, e >= l_b > r_a >= lo_a? Actually e >= l_b > r_a >= lo_a? r_a >= lo_a, so hi_a = min(r_a,e)=r_a >= lo_a. Good. For b, s <= r_a < l_b <= lo_b? lo_b = l_b, hi_b = min(r_b,e) >= l_b. Good.
For a==b, lo<=hi as argued.

Let's use general formula; code a bit longer but safe.

Now, let's think about if there is a possibility that the optimal window starts at a coordinate inside interval A and ends at a coordinate inside interval B with c_A = c_B, and A and B are not adjacent; plateau may include gaps? Now, let's consider if there is a possibility that the optimal window starts at a coordinate that is not a left endpoint and not r_i-k+1 but is a local maximum due to c_A > c_e and c_A > c_{e+1}? Let's revisit local max condition with discrete Δ. Suppose s inside A, e inside B (not right endpoint). Δ(s-1)=F(s)-F(s-1)=A(e)-A(s-1)=c_B - c_A. Δ(s)=F(s+1)-F(s)=A(e+1)-A(s)=c_B - c_A. They are equal! So if c_B > c_A, F increasing; if c_B < c_A, F decreasing; if equal, plateau. Thus no strict local max inside. If e in gap, A(e)=0, Δ(s-1)= -c_A <0, so F decreasing as s increases; moving left improves. If e at right endpoint, Δ(s)=A(e+1)-A(s)=0 - c_A = -c_A, Δ(s-1)=A(e)-A(s-1)=c_B - c_A. For local max need Δ(s-1)>=0 => c_B>=c_A. If c_B<c_A, moving left improves; if c_B>=c_A, s = r_B-k+1 candidate. If s at left endpoint, candidate. This is a clean discrete proof. It shows any strict local max must have s=l_i or e=r_i. If plateau, endpoints are such. Good. We don't need include l_i-k or r_i+1 because those are where Δ changes due to A(s) or A(s+k) but if F is increasing/decreasing through them, not max unless plateau endpoint? Let's see s=r_i+1 (start after interval) corresponds to A(s) drop, Δ decreases. It could be a local max if Δ(s-1)>0 and Δ(s)<0? Example interval [1,10] c=1, k=5, s=11? F(10)=5? Wait interval [1,10], k=5, F(10)=1? Actually s=10 [10,14] covers pos10 =1. s=11 [11,15] 0. Δ(10)=F11-F10=-1. Δ(9)=F10-F9=1-2=-1. No local max. s=11 not max. If there is a high interval after, s=r_i+1 might be before next interval; shifting right to l_next improves. So not needed. s=l_i-k (end before interval) corresponds to A(s+k) jump, Δ increases; could be local max if Δ(s-1)>0 and Δ(s)<0? Since Δ increases, cannot go from positive to negative; it goes from negative to positive (local min). So not max. Good. Thus 2 candidates sufficient and elegant.

Now, let's think about if there is a possibility that the optimal window starts at a coordinate where Δ(s-1)>0 and Δ(s)=0 (start of plateau), and s is not l_i or r_i-k+1? Now, let's think about if there is a possibility that the optimal window starts at a coordinate where Δ(s-1)=0 and Δ(s)<0 (end of plateau), and s is not l_i or r_i-k+1? Now, let's consider if there is a possibility that the optimal window starts at a coordinate where Δ(s-1)>0 and Δ(s)<0 but s = l_i-k or r_i+1? We argued Δ increases at l_i-k, decreases at r_i+1. A local max requires Δ change from positive to negative, i.e., decrease. So it could occur at r_i+1? Wait Δ(s) = A(s+k)-A(s). At s = r_i+1, A(s) drops (decreases), so Δ(s) = A(s+k)-A(s) increases (because subtract smaller A(s)). Actually if A(s) drops, Δ increases, not decreases. Let's compute: Δ(s)-Δ(s-1) = [A(s+k)-A(s)] - [A(s+k-1)-A(s-1)] = (A(s+k)-A(s+k-1)) + (A(s-1)-A(s)). At s = r_i+1, A(s) drops, so A(s-1)-A(s) = c_i - 0? Wait A(s-1)=c_i (if s-1=r_i), A(s)=0, so A(s-1)-A(s)=c_i >0. Thus Δ increases. So r_i+1 is where Δ increases, causing local min, not max. At s = l_i, A(s) rises, A(s-1)-A(s) = -c_i, so Δ decreases, local max possible. At s = r_i+1-k, A(s+k) drops, A(s+k)-A(s+k-1) = -c_i, Δ decreases, local max possible. At s = l_i-k, A(s+k) rises, Δ increases, local min. Thus local max candidates are l_i and r_i+1-k. Perfect. This is a strong proof. Let's articulate in mind:
Δ(s) changes by:
- At s = l_i: A(s-1)-A(s) = -c_i => Δ decreases. Could be max.
- At s = r_i+1: A(s-1)-A(s) = +c_i => Δ increases. Could be min.
- At s = l_i-k: A(s+k)-A(s+k-1) = +c_i => Δ increases. Could be min.
- At s = r_i+1-k: A(s+k)-A(s+k-1) = -c_i => Δ decreases. Could be max.
So only l_i and r_i+1-k can be local maxima. Great. This confirms 2 candidates.

Now, let's think about if global max at boundary of domain (s very negative/positive) where Δ doesn't change? F=0 at both ends, positive inside, so not. If all intervals? finite. Good.

```python
from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left, bisect_right

class Solution:
    def maximumCoins(self, coins: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        coins.sort(key=lambda x: x[0])
        n = len(coins)
        left = [c[0] for c in coins]
        right = [c[1] for c in coins]
        val = [c[2] for c in coins]
        
        pref = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, (l, r, c) in enumerate(coins):
            pref[i + 1] = pref[i] + (r - l + 1) * c
        
        def calc(s: int) -> int:
            e = s + k - 1
            a = bisect_left(right, s)
            if a == n:
                return 0
            b = bisect_right(left, e) - 1
            if b < a:
                return 0
            
            if a == b:
                lo = max(left[a], s)
                hi = min(right[a], e)
                return val[a] * (hi - lo + 1)
            
            total = pref[b] - pref[a + 1]
            
            lo_a = max(left[a], s)
            hi_a = min(right[a], e)
            total += val[a] * (hi_a - lo_a + 1)
            
            lo_b = max(left[b], s)
            hi_b = min(right[b], e)
            total += val[b] * (hi_b - lo_b + 1)
            
            return total
        
        ans = 0
        seen = set()
        for l, r, c in coins:
            for s in (l, r - k + 1):
                if s not in seen:
                    seen.add(s)
                    ans = max(ans, calc(s))
        return ans
```

Need consider if `r - k + 1` could be less than l? yes. If `r - k + 1` maybe not a valid start? It is valid. If `r - k + 1` is less than 1, calc handles. If problem restricts start to >=1, but as argued max still found. But if start must be >=1 and r-k+1 negative, calc might return same as l or less. No issue. If there are intervals before? l_i>=1, no intervals before 1. If r-k+1 negative and l=1, calc returns full interval maybe same as l. Good.

coins sorted. pref. candidates. ans 10. Good.

```
if lo > hi: return 0
```
Not necessary but harmless. Maybe add to be robust. In a==b, if lo>hi impossible. But adding guard doesn't hurt. In partial a/b, if a<b, lo<=hi. No need.
